Categorifying Zeta Functions for Quadratic Covers
Jon Aycock, Andrew Kobin

Abstract
In various contexts, the zeta function of an object splits into a product of L L -functions. We categorify this product formula for quadratic covers of objects in the following contexts: quadratic extensions of number fields, ramified double covers of algebraic curves, ramified double covers of topological spaces and Galois double covers of graphs. Our unified approach utilizes objective linear algebra in the abstract incidence algebra of each object, interpreted appropriately. We also provide several applications: for a hyperelliptic curve C C over a finite field, we prove a collection of combinatorial formulas relating the number of ramified, split and inert points on C C to the overall point count of C C ; and for a graph G G , we deduce analogous combinatorial formulas for the numbers of split and inert primes in a Galois double cover G ~ → G \widetilde{G}\rightarrow G . We then use the formulas for graphs to deduce asymptotic counts of cycles in supersingular isogeny graphs and certain associated dual graphs of special fibers of Shimura curves. Finally, we analyze quadratic reciprocity from the perspective of zeta functions.



1
Introduction

This paper offers a unified category theoretic perspective on factorizations of zeta functions in several contexts: number fields, curves over finite fields, Riemann surfaces, finite CW complexes and finite graphs.

Let
K/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field. Its Dedekind zeta function is defined as the formal expression

\zeta_{K}(s)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in I_{K}^{+}}\frac{1}{N(\mathfrak{a})^{s}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#\{\mathfrak{a}\mid N(\mathfrak{a})=n\}}{n^{s}}

where
s
is a complex number,
N=N_{K/\mathbb{Q}}
is the field norm and
I_{K}^{+}
denotes the set of nonzero ideals in the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
of
K
. For example,
\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(s)
is the Riemann zeta function. The formal Dirichlet series
\zeta_{K}(s)
converges for complex numbers
s
with real part greater than
1
and, like the Riemann zeta function,
\zeta_{K}(s)
admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane, satisfies a functional equation and has a well-formulated Riemann Hypothesis predicting the location of its nontrivial zeroes. Moreover, special values of
\zeta_{K}(s)
contain rich arithmetic information which remains the subject of ongoing study.

It is well-known that when
K/\mathbb{Q}
is a quadratic extension, the zeta function factors in the ring of Dirichlet series as

\zeta_{K}(s)=\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(s)L(\chi,s)

(1)

where
L(\chi,s)
is the Dirichlet
L
-function

L(\chi,s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\chi(n)}{n^{s}}

associated to the Dirichlet character
\chi=\left(\frac{D}{\cdot}\right)
, with
D
the discriminant of
K
. More generally, for any quadratic extension
K/F
, where
F
is an arbitrary number field, the zeta function of
K
splits as

\zeta_{K}(s)=\zeta_{F}(s)L(\chi,s)

(2)

where
\chi
is the unique quadratic Hecke character on the group of fractional ideals
I_{F}
of the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{F}
associated to the extension
K/F
by class field theory. More generally, every zeta function
\zeta_{K}(s)
splits as a product of
L
-functions coming from the representation theory of the Galois group of
K
.

An analogue of formula (
2
) holds for curves over finite fields. To describe this, let
\mathbb{F}_{q}
be a finite field and let
C
be an algebraic curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Its zeta function is defined as the formal power series

Z(C,t)=\exp\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#C(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})}{n}t^{n}\right]

where
\#C(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})
denotes the number of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}}
-points of
C
and
\exp(t)=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}t^{n}
is the formal exponential power series. For example, the zeta function of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
is

Z(\mathbb{A}^{1},t)=\frac{1}{1-qt}

while the zeta function of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is

Z(\mathbb{P}^{1},t)=\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-qt)}.

By the Weil Conjectures,
Z(C,t)
is a rational function, satisfies a functional equation and its zeroes and poles satisfy an analogue of the Riemann Hypothesis.

Expanding further on the rationality property, it is well-known, though not at all obvious, that when
C
is smooth, projective and geometrically integral of genus
g
, its zeta function can be written

Z(C,t)=\frac{L(C,t)}{(1-t)(1-qt)}

where
L(C,t)
is the
L
-function of
C
, a degree
2g
polynomial with integer coefficients. In other words,
Z(C,t)
factors in the ring of formal power series as

Z(C,t)=Z(\mathbb{P}^{1},t)L(C,t).

(3)

The point counting zeta function is only one generating function that encodes information about a curve
C/\mathbb{F}_{q}
. If
C
lifts to a curve
\widetilde{C}
in characteristic
0
, then the complex points of
\widetilde{C}
have the structure of a Riemann surface, whose invariants can be extracted using generating functions. For instance, the Euler characteristic is encoded by the Macdonald polynomial
M(\widetilde{C},t)=(1-t)^{-\chi(\widetilde{C})}
. In general, many formulas for
\chi
can be encoded with Macdonald polynomials; for example, when
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
is a branched cover of finite CW complexes of degree
n
,

\chi(Y)=n\chi(X)-r

where
r=r(\pi)
is the ramification number of
\pi
. Using Macdonald polynomials,

M(Y,t)=M(X,t)^{n}(1-t)^{r}.

(4)

For our purposes,
M(X,t)^{n-1}(1-t)^{r}
plays the role of a “topological
L
-function” for the cover
\pi
.

In a parallel direction, every finite graph
X
admits an analogue of the zeta function of a curve over a finite field, called its Ihara zeta function
Z(X,u)
, defined by the formula

Z(X,u)=\prod_{\gamma}\frac{1}{1-u^{\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}},

(5)

where the product is over so-called
prime cycles
in
X
(see Section
2.4
). The Ihara zeta function of a finite graph is a rational function in
u
, cf.
[
Ter
, Thm. 2.5]
, and for a
2
-to-
1
Galois cover of graphs
Y\to X
, we can factor the zeta function of
Y
as

Z(Y,u)=Z(X,u)L(X,\chi,u).

(6)

Here,
\chi
is the nontrivial character of the Galois group of
Y
over
X
, and
L(X,\chi,u)
is the corresponding
Artin–Ihara
L
-function
, which is rational as well
[
Ter
, Ch. 18]
. More generally, as with the more number-theoretic zeta functions above, the zeta function of an arbitrary Galois cover factors as a product of Artin–Ihara
L
-functions
[
Ter
, Cor. 18.11]
.

1.1
Categorification

In this paper, we generalize the above formulas using objective linear algebra (see Section
3.6
and Appendix
A
for an overview of this theory). Namely, for a double cover
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
of an object
X
(equal to
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{F}
for a number field
F
, an algebraic curve
C
over an
arbitrary
field, a finite CW complex or a finite graph), there is a formula for the zeta function (Dedekind zeta, point-counting zeta, Macdonald polynomial or Ihara zeta) of
Y
as a product of
L
-functions:

Z(Y)=Z(X)L(\pi).

(7)

The main goal of this paper is to use objective linear algebra to lift formula (
7
) to an equivalence of linear functors in the incidence algebra of an appropriate simplicial set
S(X)
of “effective
0
-cycles” for
X
:

Theorem 1.1
.

In the incidence algebra
I(S(X))
, there is an equivalence of linear functors

\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y}+\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{-}\cong\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{+}.

Here, the choice of
S(X)
depends on the object
X
:

•

when
X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{F}
for a number field
F
,
S(X)
is the monoid of ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
under multiplication;

•

when
X
is an algebraic curve,
S(X)=Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)
, the usual monoid of effective algebraic
0
-cycles on
X
under addition;

•

when
X
is a finite CW complex,
S(X)
is the simplicial complex
S_{\bullet}(X)
;

•

when
X
is a graph,
S(X)
is the monoid of “effective
0
-cycles” - similar to the case of curves, this is the free abelian monoid generated by the prime cycles (see Section
2.4
).

In Theorem
1.1
, the symbol
\pi_{*}
is a pushforward map induced by the double cover
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
. The functors
L(\pi)^{+}
and
L(\pi)^{-}
in
\widetilde{I}(S(X))
are defined in Section
4
in such a way that their images in the numerical incidence algebra satisfy
L(\pi)^{+}-L(\pi)^{-}=L(\pi)
, where
L(\pi)
represents the coefficients of each of the “
L
-functions” in formulas (
2
), (
3
), (
4
) and (
6
). Decategorifying in the right way recovers each of these formulas, as well as many others; see Remarks
4.6
and
4.7
.

Theorem
1.1
also says that
L(\pi)
is a
relative zeta function
for the cover
\pi
, in the terminology of
[
AK1
]
. Or to be more precise, it is a “decomposed” relative zeta function and becomes a relative zeta function after passing to the numerical incidence algebra
I_{\#}(S(X))
; see Remark
4.5
. We plan to construct
L(\pi)
directly as a single relative zeta function in a future article, using simplicial
G
-representations. See Appendix
A.4
for an overview of the construction in the case that
X
is an elliptic curve.

Finally, in Subsection
4.4
, we give the following motivic analogue of Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
4.13
)
.

For a double cover of curves
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
over a field
k
, in the incidence algebra
I_{mot}(X)
of the decomposition scheme
S^{\bullet}X
associated to the monoid scheme
\coprod_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}^{n}X
, there is an equivalence of linear functors

\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y}+\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{-}\cong\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{+}.

1.2
Applications

When
E
is an elliptic curve over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, the
L
-function of
E
is of the form
L(E,t)=1-a_{q}t+qt^{2}
where
a_{q}=q+1-\#E(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Using Theorem
1.1
, we deduce the formulas

a_{q}=i_{q}(E)-s_{q}(E)\quad\text{and}\quad\#\operatorname{Sym}^{n}C(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\sum_{i+j=n}\left[(q^{i}+\ldots+q+1)\sum_{\alpha\in\operatorname{Sym}^{j}\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\chi(\alpha)\right]

(8)

where
s_{q}(E)
and
i_{q}(E)
are the number of “split” and “inert” points of the cover
E\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and
\chi
is a certain function on the points of
\operatorname{Sym}^{j}\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which can be identified with the effective
0
-cycles of degree
j
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. It is possible to deduce more complicated formulas for higher genus hyperelliptic curves in a similar fashion.

Analogously, when
Y\xrightarrow{\pi}X
is a Galois double cover of finite graphs, the ratio
\zeta_{Y}(u)/\zeta_{X}(u)
of the graphs’ Ihara zeta functions is an
Artin–Ihara
L
-function

L(\pi,u)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f(n)u^{n}
whose coefficients are determined by

f(n)=\sum_{\operatorname{len}(\alpha)=n}\chi(\alpha)

(9)

where the sum is over all effective
0
-cycles
\alpha
on
X
and
\chi
is a quadratic character on the primes of
X
with values determined by the number of split and inert primes in the cover
\pi
. As with hyperelliptic curves, formula (
9
) allows one to quickly compute loop counts in
Y
from information stored on
X
.

As a special case of this counting technique on graphs, we consider the graph
G_{p}(\ell)
of supersingular
\ell
-isogenies, whose vertices are supersingular elliptic curves over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
and whose edges correspond to
\ell
-isogenies, for
\ell
a prime distinct from
p
. Work of Ribet
[
Rib
]
shows that
G_{p}(\ell)
admits a double cover by the dual graph
\widetilde{G}
of the special fiber of a certain Shimura curve. We use formula (
9
) to recover known asymptotics (cf.
[
ACL+
]
) for
G_{p}(\ell)
and extend them to
\widetilde{G}
:

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
5.13
, Corollary
5.14
)
.

For distinct primes
p
and
\ell
, let
G=G_{p}(\ell)
be the graph of supersingular
\ell
-isogenies over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
and let
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{G}_{p}(\ell)
be the dual graph to the special fiber at
\ell
of the Shimura curve
X_{0}^{p\ell}(1)
. When
p\equiv 1\pmod{12}
, the number of isogeny cycles of length
m
in
G
grows asymptotically as
\ell^{m}/2m
and, if
m\geq 2
is even, the number of prime cycles of length
m
in
\widetilde{G}
grows asymptotically as
\ell^{m}/m
.

1.3
Relation to Previous Work

This paper subsumes the authors’ previous papers
[
AK1
]
and
[
AK2
]
on quadratic number fields and hyperelliptic curves, respectively, and extends the techniques to new situations, notably Galois covers of finite graphs.

1.4
Organization

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we recall basic properties of number fields, algebraic curves over finite fields, finite CW complexes and finite graphs, as well as each of their zeta and
L
-functions. In Section
3
, we review the definitions of decomposition sets and their incidence algebras, following
[
GKT1
,
Kob
]
. The proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
are given in Section
4
. In Section
5
we prove formula (
8
) relating the numbers of split and inert points of
E
to
a_{q}(E)
for an elliptic curve
E/\mathbb{F}_{q}
, as well as its graph theoretic analogue (
9
) for a double cover of graphs
Y\rightarrow X
. Section
5.3
gives a brief overview of supersingular isogeny graphs and a proof of Theorem
1.3
. Finally, in Section
5.4
, we describe quadratic reciprocity in the language of zeta functions of quadratic covers.

The authors would like to thank their team members at the first Rethinking Number Theory (RNT) workshop - Karen Acquista, Changho Han and Alicia Lamarche - for their early contributions to this project and for many conversations along the way. The authors are grateful to the organizers of RNT - Heidi Goodson, Christelle Vincent and Mckenzie West - for the opportunity to begin this project, as well as to all the RNT participants. Finally, the second author would like to thank David Zureick-Brown for numerous suggestions that improved the article, Aly Deines for many productive discussions, and Bogdan Krstic for his help with the details of objective characteristic polynomials.

2
Double Covers

In this section, we survey four different types of finite covers: finite extensions of number fields, superelliptic curves (and more generally Galois covers of curves), topological covering spaces and Galois covers of graphs. In each setting, there is a natural notion of a zeta function which splits as a product of
L
-functions. We describe this splitting explicitly for double covers.

2.1
Quadratic Number Fields

Let
K/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, norm map
N=N_{K/\mathbb{Q}}
and Dedekind zeta function
\zeta_{K}(s)
. Then
\zeta_{K}(s)
has the following product formula:

\zeta_{K}(s)=\prod_{\mathfrak{p}}\frac{1}{1-N(\mathfrak{p})^{-s}}.

Here, the product is over all prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}
. We denote the set of all ideals in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
by
I_{K}^{+}
to distinguish it from
I_{K}
, which is commonly used for the group of fractional ideals.

Now assume
K/F
is an extension of number fields. For a fixed prime ideal
p\in I_{F}^{+}
and a fixed prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\in I_{K}^{+}
lying over
p
, let
e(\mathfrak{p}\mid p)
and
f(\mathfrak{p}\mid p)
denote the ramification index and inertia degree of
\mathfrak{p}
, respectively. Recall that
p
has one of the following splitting types:

•

ramified
if
e(\mathfrak{p}\mid p)>1
for some
\mathfrak{p}
lying over
p
;

•

split
if
e(\mathfrak{p}\mid p)=f(\mathfrak{p}\mid p)=1
for all
\mathfrak{p}
lying over
p
;

•

inert
if
p\mathcal{O}_{K}
is itself a prime ideal.

When
F=\mathbb{Q}
and the splitting types of all primes in the extension
K/\mathbb{Q}
are known, one can factor
\zeta_{K}(s)
as a Dirichlet series, that is, into local factors at each prime. This can also be done relatively, i.e. in an extension
K/F
with
F
arbitrary. We state this for quadratic extensions below.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
K/F
be a quadratic extension of number fields. Then
\zeta_{K}(s)
has the following prime factorization “over
F
”:

\zeta_{K}(s)=\prod_{p\in I_{F}^{+}}\zeta_{K,p}(s)

where

\zeta_{K,p}(s)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-s}},&\text{if $p$ is ramified}\\[10.00002pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-s})^{2}},&\text{if $p$ is split}\\[10.00002pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-2s}},&\text{if $p$ is inert}.\end{cases}

We can view each of these local factors as a generating function for ideals lying over
p
:

\displaystyle\zeta_{K,p}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\#\{\mathfrak{a}\in I_{F}^{+}\mid N_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{a})=N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{n}\}p^{-ns}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1+N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-s}+N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-2s}+\ldots,&\text{if $p$ is ramified}\\
1+2N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-s}+3N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-2s}+\ldots,&\text{if $p$ is split}\\
1+N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-2s}+N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(p)^{-4s}+\ldots,&\text{if $p$ is inert}.\end{cases}

Example 2.2
.

A well-known example is when
K=\mathbb{Q}(i)
. In this case,

p\text{ is }\begin{cases}&\text{ramified when }p=2\\
&\text{split when }p\equiv 1\!\pmod{4}\\
&\text{inert when }p\equiv 3\!\pmod{4}.\end{cases}

This means the zeta function of
\mathbb{Q}(i)
can be written

\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}(i)}(s)=\frac{1}{1-2^{-s}}\times\prod_{p\equiv 1\,(4)}\frac{1}{(1-p^{-s})^{2}}\times\prod_{p\equiv 3\,(4)}\frac{1}{1-p^{-2s}}.

2.2
Hyperelliptic Curves

The above description of zeta functions of quadratic extensions applies equally well in the setting of function fields. In this section, we describe the corresponding geometric picture, i.e. using the point-counting zeta function of curves over a finite field.

For a smooth, projective, geometrically integral variety
X
over
k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
, let
|X|
denote the set of closed points of
X
. Then the zeta function of
X
has the following product formula:

Z(X,t)=\prod_{x\in|X|}\frac{1}{1-t^{\deg(x)}}

where
\deg(x)=[k(x):k]
is the degree of
x
. We denote by
Z_{0}(X)
the group of
0
-cycles on
X
, i.e. the free abelian group generated by
|X|
. A
0
-cycle is
effective
if it is of the form
\alpha=\sum_{x\in|X|}a_{x}x
for
a_{x}\geq 0
. The effective
0
-cycles on
X
form a submonoid of
Z_{0}(X)
, denoted
Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)
. The zeta function of
X
can also be written as a generating function for the effective
0
-cycles on
X
:

Z(X,t)=\sum_{\alpha\in Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)}t^{\deg(\alpha)}

where
\deg:Z_{0}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is the degree map sending
\alpha=\sum_{x}a_{x}x
to
\deg(\alpha)=\sum_{x}a_{x}{\deg(x)}
.

Remark 2.3
.

By the number field/function field analogy, one can treat
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{K}
as an arithmetic curve and define
Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{K})
the same way, producing a monoid which is isomorphic to
I_{K}
. In fact, the effective
0
-cycles approach works for any scheme, so it should be considered the more natural choice.

Let
C
be a hyperelliptic curve of genus
g\geq 1
, i.e. a smooth, projective algebraic curve given by an equation of the form

C:y^{2}+h(x)y=f(x)

for
f,h\in k[x]
with
f
monic,
\deg(f)=2g+1
and
\deg(h)\leq g
. (If
\operatorname{char}k\not=2
, we can take
h=0
.) Restricting the projection
\mathbb{P}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
to
C
determines a smooth map
\pi:C\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
which is a degree
2
cover ramified at
2g+2
points.

Remark 2.4
.

Although it is not standard everywhere, in this paper we include elliptic curves in the class of hyperelliptic curves.

Fix a point
x\in|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
and a point
y\in\pi^{-1}(x)
on
C
. Let
e(y\mid x)
denote the ramification index of
\pi
at
y
and let
f(y\mid x)
denote the inertia degree, defined as
[k(y):k(x)]
where
k(x)
(resp.
k(y)
) is the field of definition of
x
(resp.
y
). Then
x
has one of the following splitting types:

•

ramified
if
e(y\mid x)>1
for any
y\in\pi^{-1}(x)
;

•

split
if
e(y\mid x)=f(y\mid x)=1
for all
y\in\pi^{-1}(x)
;

•

inert
if
\pi^{-1}(x)
consists of a single closed point
y
and
e(y\mid x)=1
.

In this situation, the Riemann–Hurwitz formula reads

2-2g=4-\sum_{y\in|C|}(e(y\mid x)-1).

Compare this to the formulas in Section
2.3
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\pi:C\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a hyperelliptic curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Then
Z(C,t)
factors as

Z(C,t)=\prod_{x\in|\mathbb{P}^{1}|}Z_{x}(C,t)

where

Z_{x}(C,t)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-t^{\deg(x)}},&\text{if $x$ is ramified}\\[10.00002pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-t^{\deg(x)})^{2}},&\text{if $x$ is split}\\[10.00002pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-t^{2\deg(x)}},&\text{if $x$ is inert}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The factorization of
Z(C,t)
as a product over the closed points of
C
is well known:

Z(C,t)=\prod_{y\in|C|}\frac{1}{1-t^{\deg(y)}}.

For each
x\in|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
, write

Z_{x}(C,t)=\prod_{y\in\pi^{-1}(x)}\frac{1}{1-t^{\deg(y)}}.

Then the description of
Z_{x}(C,t)
follows from the definitions of ramified, split and inert points. This will also follow from the objective description of
Z(C,t)
as the pushforward
\pi_{*}\zeta_{C}
(see Section
5
).
∎

2.3
Topological Covers

By the function field/Riemann surface analogy, we can replace
C/\mathbb{F}_{q}
with a complex algebraic curve
C/\mathbb{C}
, or equivalently with a Riemann surface
X=C^{\operatorname{an}}
. Then (ramified) covers of algebraic curves correspond to (branched) covers of Riemann surfaces. Everything that follows works in the more general setting of finite-sheeted branched covers of finite CW complexes, so for the rest of this section, assume
X
and
Y
are finite CW complexes.

First, suppose
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
is an unbranched cover of degree
n\geq 2
. Then the Euler characteristics of
X
and
Y
satisfy the following well-known relation:

\chi(Y)=n\chi(X).

(10)

If
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
is a branched cover, the relation between
\chi(X)
and
\chi(Y)
is

\chi(Y)=n\chi(X)-r,

where
r
is the ramification number of the cover. Call the irreducible components of the branch locus
X_{1},\ldots,X_{r}\subset X_{br}
and for each component
Z_{i}\subseteq\pi^{-1}(X_{i})
, let
e(Y_{i})
be the ramification index of
X_{i}
. Setting
e(Z)=1
for each irreducible component
Z\subseteq Y,Z\not\subseteq\pi^{-1}(X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{r})
, we have

\sum_{Z\subseteq\pi^{-1}(X^{\prime})}e(Z)=n

for
any
irreducible component
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
. Then the precise analogue of formula (
10
) for branched covers is

\chi(Y)=n\chi(X)-\sum_{Z\subseteq Y}(e(Z)-1).

(11)

That is,
r=\sum(e(Z)-1)
.

2.4
Galois Covers of Graphs

Let
X=(V,\vec{E},o,t,\iota)
be a finite, connected (bidirected) graph, where
V
is a set of vertices,
\vec{E}
is a set of directed edges,
o,t\colon\vec{E}\to V
are the “origin” and “terminus” functions and
\iota\colon\vec{E}\to\vec{E}
is an involution
1
1

1

Classically, the involution is assumed to be fixed-point free, but we want to allow half-loops.
satisfying
o(\iota(e))=t(e)
and
t(\iota(e))=o(e)
for all
e\in\vec{E}
. We think of the pair
e
and
\iota(e)
as a single undirected edge connecting the vertices
o(e)
and
t(e)
. Note that
e=\iota(e)
is possible under this definition, in which case
o(e)=t(e)
and
e
is referred to as a half-loop.

A path of length
\ell
on
X
is an ordered list
\gamma
of edges
[e_{1}\cdots e_{\ell}]
with
o(e_{i+1})=t(e_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq\ell
. We say the path is
closed
if
o(e_{1})=t(e_{\ell})
;
reduced
if
e_{i+1}\neq\iota(e_{i})
for all
i
and
e_{1}\neq\iota(e_{\ell})
; and it is a power of another path if it is just a shorter list
[e_{1}\dots e_{\ell_{0}}]
repeated some integer number of times. We say two closed, reduced paths are equivalent if they are related under the equivalence relation generated by the relations
[e_{1}\cdots e_{\ell}]\sim[e_{2}\cdots e_{\ell}e_{1}]
.

A
prime
on
X
is an equivalence class of closed, reduced paths which are not powers of another path. Note that the lengths of primes are well-defined, since each equivalence class only contains paths of a single length. In addition, for each prime
\gamma=[e_{1}\cdots e_{\ell}]
, there is an opposite prime of the same length,
\iota(\gamma)=[\iota(e_{\ell})\cdots\iota(e_{1})]
.

We can then define the
Ihara zeta function
of a graph
X
in terms of the following product formula:

Z(X,u)=\prod_{\gamma\text{ prime}}\dfrac{1}{1-u^{\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}}.

(12)

In analogy with the point-counting zeta function for varieties over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, the Ihara zeta function counts closed paths in
X
. Explicitly,

Z(X,u)=\exp\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{N_{X}(\ell)}{\ell}u^{\ell}\right]

where
N_{X}(\ell)
denotes the number of closed paths in
X
of length
\ell
without backtracks or tails
[
Ter
, 4.5]
.

The Ihara zeta function has an alternative description as a (reciprocal) characteristic polynomial

Z(X,u)=\frac{1}{\det(1-uT_{X})},

where
T_{X}
is the
edge adjacency matrix
for
X

[
Ter
, Cor. 11.5]
. In particular,
Z(X,u)
is rational.

Let
X=(V_{X},\vec{E}_{X},o,t,\iota)
and
Y=(V_{Y},\vec{E}_{Y},o,y,\iota)
be two graphs. A morphism
f\colon Y\to X
is a pair of functions
f_{E}\colon\vec{E}_{Y}\to\vec{E}_{X}
and
f_{V}\colon V_{Y}\to V_{X}
satisfying the relations
o(f_{E}(e))=f_{V}(o(e))
,
t(f_{E}(e))=f_{V}(t(e))
, and
\iota(f_{E}(e))=f_{E}(\iota(e))
for all
e\in\vec{E}_{Y}
.

For each vertex
v\in V_{Y}
, let
E_{Y}^{+}(v)=\{e\in\vec{E}_{Y}\mid o(e)=v\}
. We say that a morphism
f\colon Y\to X
is a
covering map
if
f_{V}
is surjective and, for each
v\in V_{Y}
,
f_{E}
induces an isomorphism
E_{Y}^{+}(v)\to E_{X}^{+}(f_{V}(v))
. If, in addition, the functions
f_{E}
and
f_{V}
are both
d
-to-
1
, we say that
f
is a cover of degree
d
.

Given a prime
\gamma=[e_{1}\dots e_{\ell}]
in
X
and a degree
2
covering map
f:Y\rightarrow X
, there are two possibilities for its splitting type: we say
\gamma
is

•

split
if, for each representative
e_{1}\dots e_{\ell}
of
\gamma
, there are two distinct closed, reduced paths of length
\ell

\tilde{e}_{1,1}\dots\tilde{e}_{\ell,1}
and
\tilde{e}_{1,2}\dots\tilde{e}_{\ell,2}
in
Y
with
f_{E}(\tilde{e}_{i,j})=e_{i}
for each
i,j
;

•

inert
if, for each representative
e_{1}\dots e_{\ell}
of
\gamma
and each choice of an edge
\tilde{e}_{1,1}
with
f_{E}(\tilde{e}_{1,1})
, there is a unique closed, reduced path of length
2\ell
,
\tilde{e}_{1,1}\dots\tilde{e}_{\ell,1}\tilde{e}_{1,2}\dots\tilde{e}_{\ell,2}
in
Y
, with
f_{E}(\tilde{e}_{i,j})=e_{i}
for each
i,j
and
\tilde{e}_{i,1}\neq\tilde{e}_{i,2}
for each
i
. In particular, there will be no
closed
paths in
Y
that lift
e_{1}\dots e_{\ell}
, but two distinct closed paths in
Y
which lift the closed path
e_{1}\dots e_{\ell}e_{1}\dots e_{\ell}
: one starting with
\tilde{e}_{1,1}
and one starting with
\tilde{e}_{1,2}
.

There is no ramification in this setting (although see Section
6
). We can factor
Z(Y,u)
into a product over the primes of
X
as in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a double cover of graphs. Then
Z(Y,u)
has the following prime factorization “over
X
”:

Z(Y,u)=\prod_{\gamma}Z_{\gamma}(Y,u)

(13)

where the product is over all primes
\gamma
in
X
and

Z_{\gamma}(Y,u)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{\left(1-u^{\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}\right)^{2}},&\text{if }\gamma\text{ is split}\\[10.00002pt]
\dfrac{1}{1-u^{2\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}},&\text{if }\gamma\text{ is inert.}\end{cases}

(14)

When
f\colon Y\to X
is a quadratic cover of graphs and
\gamma
is a prime of
X
that splits in
Y
, we label the two primes of
Y
that lie over
\gamma
as
\eta
and
\overline{\eta}
. The formulas proven in Section
4
will not depend on this labeling, although their proofs will.

3
Incidence Algebras and Objective Linear Algebra

In this section, we define an incidence algebra of
0
-cycles in each of our four settings: number fields, varieties over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, finite CW complexes and graphs. For most of these, this is accomplished by first attaching a monoid to each object, then invoking a general construction of incidence algebras for monoids, which is reviewed below. To integrate CW complexes, we attach a more general simplicial set, which then requires the more general theory of decomposition sets, objective linear algebra and objective incidence algebras. This theory is reviewed in Subsections
3.5
,
3.6
and
3.7
, following
[
GKT1
,
Kob
]
.

3.1
Incidence Algebras for Monoids

Fix a field
k
. We say a monoid
M
is
locally finite
if for every
x\in M
, there are only finitely many ways of factoring
x=ab
for
a,b\in M
.

Following
[
GKT1
]
and
[
Kob
]
, the incidence algebra of a locally finite monoid is defined by first defining its incidence coalgebra, then taking the dual.

Definition 3.1
.

The
incidence coalgebra
of a locally free monoid
M
with unit
1
is the free
k
-vector space

C(M)=\bigoplus_{x\in M}kx

together with comultiplication and counit

\displaystyle\Delta:C(M)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow C(M)\otimes_{k}C(M)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{ab=x}a\otimes b

\displaystyle\delta:C(M)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow k

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\delta_{x1}

where
\delta_{xy}
is the Kronecker delta symbol. Dually, the
incidence algebra
of
M
is the
k
-algebra
I(M)=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(C(M),k)
, with multiplication defined by extensions

\displaystyle*:I(M)\otimes_{k}I(M)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow I(M)

\displaystyle f\otimes g

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left(f*g:x\mapsto\sum_{ab=x}f(a)g(b)\right)

(extended linearly) and unit
\delta\in I(M)
.

For a locally finite monoid
M
, the incidence algebra
I(M)
is always associative and unital; however, it need not be commutative.

There is a distinguished element
\zeta\in I(M)
, called the
zeta function
of
M
, defined by
\zeta(x)=1
for all
x\in M
. The principle of
Möbius inversion

[
Rot
,
GKT2
]
says that
\zeta
is an invertible element of
I(M)
, whose inverse is called the
Möbius function
and is defined by the following recursion:

\zeta^{-1}=\mu:x\longmapsto\begin{cases}1,&x=1\\
-\sum_{ab=x}\mu(a),&x\not=1.\end{cases}

Perhaps unsurprisingly, the zeta functions in our different settings correspond to zeta elements in the incidence algebras of certain monoids, which we define in the next few sections.

Alternatively, these incidence algebras can be constructed by attaching a poset to our objects and taking the
reduced incidence algebra
of this poset. By the general theory of decomposition sets, decalage and CULF functors, these two approaches yield the same incidence algebra; cf.
[
GKT1
,
GKT5
,
Kob
]
.

3.2
The Incidence Algebra of a Number Field

Let
K/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. The set of ideals in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
forms a locally finite monoid under multiplication, written
I_{K}^{\times}
. When
K=\mathbb{Q}
, this is just the monoid of natural numbers
\mathbb{N}^{\times}
. Let
I(K)=I(I_{K}^{\times})
be the incidence algebra of this monoid.

For
K=\mathbb{Q}
, the incidence algebra
I(\mathbb{Q})=I(\mathbb{N}^{\times})
is isomorphic to the algebra of formal Dirichlet series:

\displaystyle I(\mathbb{N}^{\times})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}DS(k)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{f(n)}{n^{s}}

and the zeta element
\zeta\in\widetilde{I}(\mathbb{N},\mid)
corresponds to the Riemann zeta function:

\zeta\longleftrightarrow\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{s}}.

For a number field
K/\mathbb{Q}
, the field norm induces a map of monoids
N:I_{K}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}^{\times}
. The resulting map
I(K)\rightarrow I(\mathbb{Q})\cong DS(k)
can be used to construct a Dirichlet series for any
f\in I(K)
:

f\longmapsto F(s)=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in I_{K}^{\times}}\frac{f(\mathfrak{a})}{N(\mathfrak{a})^{s}}.

In particular, the zeta element
\zeta\in I(K)
maps to the Dedekind zeta function of
K
under this norm map:

\zeta\longmapsto\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\in I_{K}^{\times}}\frac{1}{N(\mathfrak{a})^{s}}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\#N^{-1}(n)}{n^{s}}.

More generally, for an extension of number fields
K/F
, there is a notion of pushforward on incidence algebras which lifts this Dirichlet series machine to the relative setting (see Example
3.7
).

3.3
The Incidence Algebra of Effective
0
-Cycles for a Variety

Next, we construct the incidence algebra of effective
0
-cycles for a variety
X
over a field
F
. Let
Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)
be the set of effective
0
-cycles on
X
. This has the structure of a locally finite monoid under addition, so we can apply the construction from Section
3.1
. Fix a field of coefficients
k
(usually
k=\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
for
\ell\nmid q
). The
incidence algebra of effective
0
-cycles
of
X
, hereafter the incidence algebra of
X
, is

I(X):=I(Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X))=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(C(Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)),k),

with convolution product

(f*g)(\alpha)=\sum_{\beta+\gamma=\alpha}f(\beta)g(\gamma).

The
zeta function
of
X
is the element
\zeta\in I(X)
defined by
\zeta(\alpha)=1
for every
\alpha\in Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)
.

Example 3.2
.

When
F=\mathbb{F}_{q}
and
X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}_{q})
is isomorphic to the monoid
\mathbb{N}_{0}
under addition and as a result, its incidence algebra is isomorphic to the ring of power series:

I(\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{F}_{q})\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}k[[t]],\quad f\longmapsto\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f(n)t^{n}.

More generally, for any variety
X/\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
I(X)
is isomorphic to a completed tensor product of power series rings (see Appendix
B
):

I(X)\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\widehat{\bigotimes_{x\in|X|}}k[[t_{x}]],\quad f\longmapsto\bigotimes_{x\in|X|}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f(nx)t_{x}^{n}.

Notice that under the map
\widehat{\bigotimes}_{x\in|X|}k[[t_{x}]]\rightarrow k[[t]]
induced by
t_{x}\mapsto t^{\deg(x)}
,
\zeta\in I(X)
is sent to the usual point-counting zeta function
Z(X,t)
:

\zeta=\bigotimes_{x\in|X|}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t_{x}^{n}\longmapsto\prod_{x\in|X|}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t^{n\deg(x)}=\prod_{x\in|X|}\frac{1}{1-t^{\deg(x)}}=Z(X,t).

This mapping
\widehat{\bigotimes}_{x\in|X|}k[[t_{x}]]\rightarrow k[[t]]
, which constructs a generating function for every
f\in I(X)
, is another special case of a pushforward between incidence algebras (see Example
3.7
). We will see that formula (
3
) is a statement about the pushforward of
\zeta\in\widetilde{I}(C)
along a map
C\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

3.4
The Incidence Algebra of Effective
0
-cycles for a Graph

Similarly, for a graph
X
, let
Z_{0}^{\text{eff}}(X)=\mathbb{N}_{0}\{\text{primes of }X\}
be the free commutative monoid generated by the primes of
X
. This is a locally finite monoid, so we can apply the construction from Section
3.1
. The
incidence algebra of effective
0
-cycles
of
X
, hereafter the incidence algebra of
X
, is

I(X):=I(Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X))=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(C(Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)),k),

with convolution product

(f*g)(\alpha)=\sum_{\beta+\gamma=\alpha}f(\beta)g(\gamma).

The
zeta function
of
X
is the element
\zeta\in I(X)
defined by
\zeta(\alpha)=1
for every
\alpha\in Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)
.

Example 3.3
.

When
X=C_{\ell}
is a cycle graph, meaning a connected graph with
\ell
vertices, each connected to the next in a cycle by
\ell
undirected edges, then
X
has only two primes, which we will denote
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\iota}
. Thus
Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(C_{\ell})
is isomorphic to the monoid
\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
under addition and as a result, its incidence algebra is isomorphic to the ring of power series:

I(C_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}k[[t_{1},t_{2}]],\quad f\longmapsto\sum_{a,b\geq 0}f(a\gamma+b\gamma^{\iota})t_{1}^{a}t_{2}^{b}.

More generally,
I(X)
is a completed tensor product of power series rings, as in Section
3.3
(again, see Appendix
B
). Likewise, formula (
6
) is a consequence of the pushforward construction of Example
3.7
applied to a double cover
Y\rightarrow X
.

3.5
Decomposition Sets

It is possible to construct incidence algebras for a far more general class of objects than monoids. For example, Leroux and others
[
Ler
,
CLL
,
Dür
]
construct incidence algebras for so-called Möbius categories, which include locally finite monoids (and posets) as a special case via their nerves. Other examples of naturally-occurring incidence algebras include: the Hopf algebra of rooted trees
[
But
,
CK
]
, the Faà di Bruno bialgebra
[
Joy
,
Dür
]
and the Hall algebra of an exact category
[
DK
]
.

All of the above situations have been subsumed by the concept of a
decomposition space
, introduced and studied by Gálvez-Carrillo, Kock and Tonks in
[
GKT1
,
GKT2
,
GKT3
,
GKT4
,
GKT5
]
. In this paper, we will only need the notion of a
decomposition set
, so in this section we will explain the relevant definitions and properties from
[
GKT1
]
in the category of simplicial sets. See Appendix
A
for a brief summary of the general theory.

Fix a field of coefficients
k
and let
S
be a simplicial set, with face maps
d_{i}:S_{n}\rightarrow S_{n-1}
and degeneracy maps
s_{i}:S_{n}\rightarrow S_{n+1}
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
S:\Delta^{op}\rightarrow{\tt Set}
be a simplicial set which is locally of finite length (cf.
[
GKT2
]
for a precise definition). The
numerical incidence coalgebra
of
S
is the free
k
-vector space
C(S)
on the set
S_{1}
of
1
-simplices of
S
, together with the comultiplication map

\displaystyle\Delta:C(S)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow C(S)\otimes C(S)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in S_{2}\\
d_{1}\sigma=x\end{subarray}}d_{2}\sigma\otimes d_{0}\sigma

and counit

\displaystyle\delta:C(S)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow k

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $x$ is degenerate}\\
0,&\text{if $x$ is nondegenerate}.\end{cases}

The
numerical incidence algebra
of
S
is the dual vector space
I(S)=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(C(S),k)
together with the multiplication map

\displaystyle I(S)\otimes I(S)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow I(S)

\displaystyle f\otimes g

\displaystyle\longmapsto\left(f*g:x\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in S_{2}\\
d_{1}\sigma=x\end{subarray}}f(d_{2}\sigma)g(d_{0}\sigma)\right)

and unit
\delta
.

To distinguish it from the abstract incidence algebra to be defined below, we will sometimes denote the numerical incidence algebra of
S
by
I_{\#}(S)
.

For any simplicial set
S
, the
zeta function
of
S
is the element
\zeta\in I(S)
defined by
\zeta:x\mapsto 1
for all
x\in S_{1}
and extended linearly. In general,
I(S)
is neither associative nor unital as a
k
-algebra. However, when
S
is a
decomposition set
,
I(S)
is an associative, unital
k
-algebra
[
GKT1
, Sec. 5.3]
. Following
loc. cit.
, we say a morphism
g:[m]\rightarrow[n]
in the category
\Delta
is
active
if
g(0)=0
and
g(m)=n
, and we say
g
is
inert
if
g(i+1)=g(i)+1
for all
0\leq i\leq m-1
.

Definition 3.5
.

A
decomposition set
is a simplicial set
S:\Delta^{op}\rightarrow{\tt Set}
that takes any pushout diagram in
\Delta
of the form
[p]
[m]
[\ell]
[n]
f
g

where
f
is inert and
g
is active, to a pullback diagram in
{\tt Set}
:
S_{p}
S_{m}
S_{\ell}
S_{n}
f^{*}
g^{*}

Note that locally finite monoids are examples of
Möbius categories
, which are special types of decomposition sets. Therefore all incidence algebras constructed so far are associative and unital.

3.6
Objective Linear Algebra

Informally, objective linear algebra is “linear algebra with sets”. In lieu of a full description of the theory, which already appears in
[
GKT4
,
Kob
,
AK1
]
, here is a dictionary of some linear algebra terms and their objective counterparts.

Linear

Objective

field of scalars
k

the category
{\tt Set}

scalar addition
+

coproduct
\coprod

scalar multiplication

product
\times

a basis
B

a set
B

a vector
v
in the basis
B

a set map
v:X\rightarrow B

the vector space with basis
B

the slice category
{\tt Set}_{/B}

vector addition
v+w

coproduct
v\amalg w:X\coprod Y\rightarrow B

scalar multiplication
av

A\times(v:X\rightarrow B):=(A\times X\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}\times v}A\times B\xrightarrow{\operatorname{proj}_{B}}B)

a matrix
M

a span
B
C
M
v
w

the linear map with matrix
M

the linear functor
w_{!}v^{*}:{\tt Set}_{/B}\rightarrow{\tt Set}_{/C}

matrix multiplication

span composition

tensor product
V\otimes W

symmetric tensor
{\tt Set}_{/B}\otimes{\tt Set}_{/C}:={\tt Set}_{/B\times C}

dual space
V^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}(V,k)

functor space
({\tt Set}_{/B})^{*}:=\operatorname{Fun}({\tt Set}_{/B},{\tt Set})

Here, for any span (as pictured), the composition
w_{!}v^{*}
in the definition of linear functor consists of the pullback functor
v^{*}:{\tt Set}_{/B}\rightarrow{\tt Set}_{/M},(X\rightarrow B)\mapsto(X\times_{B}M\rightarrow M)
and the pushforward functor
w_{!}:{\tt Set}_{/M}\rightarrow{\tt Set}_{/C},(Y\rightarrow M)\mapsto(Y\rightarrow M\xrightarrow{w}C)
.

To recover the objects in the left column of the table, one can often apply the cardinality functor to objects in the right column. For example, if
v:X\rightarrow B
is an objective vector with finite fibres, then the collection of its fibre cardinalities defines a vector
\sum_{b\in B}|v^{-1}(b)|b
in the
k
-vector space spanned by
B
.

3.7
The Objective Incidence Algebra

Abstract incidence algebras are defined at the objective level for any
locally finite decomposition set
in
[
GKT1
]
. For such a decomposition set
S
, the incidence algebra
I(S)
is an associative, unital monoid object in the objective linear algebra category
{\tt LIN}
. In particular, in each of our four settings the associated simplicial set is a locally finite decomposition set and therefore admits an objective incidence algebra.

Definition 3.6
.

For a simplicial set
S
, the
incidence coalgebra
of
S
is the objective vector space
C(S)={\tt Set}_{/S_{1}}
equipped with a comultiplication linear functor
\Delta:C(S)\rightarrow C(S)\otimes C(S)
, represented by the span

\Delta=\left(\hbox to86.16pt{\vbox to43.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.38298pt\lower-7.49966pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

is the dual objective vector space

, equipped with unit

defined as follows. For
f,g\in I(S)
, their product
m(f,g)
is the composition
m(f,g):{\tt Set}_{/S_{1}}\xrightarrow{\Delta}{\tt Set}_{/S_{1}}\otimes{\tt Set}_{/S_{1}}\xrightarrow{f\otimes g}{\tt Set}\otimes{\tt Set}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\tt Set}

where the top square is a pullback square.

When
S
is a decomposition set,
[
GKT1
, Thm. 7.4]
shows that
I(S)
is an associative, unital monoid object in the category of objective vector spaces and linear functors; that is,
I(S)
is an objective algebra over
{\tt Set}
. The span

\zeta=\left(\hbox to71.67pt{\vbox to43.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 37.38298pt\lower-7.49966pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

.

If

is of the form
F=G_{1}
for a simplicial map
G:T\rightarrow S
, we will write
F_{*}
or
G_{*}
to mean this pushforward of spans.

3.8
The Incidence Algebra for a CW Complex

For a CW complex
X
, let
S_{\bullet}(X)
be the associated simplicial complex, which is a decomposition set by construction. We define the incidence algebra for
X
to be incidence algebra of
S_{\bullet}(X)
. The numerical incidence algebra in this case is the
k
-algebra
I_{\#}(X)=I_{\#}(S_{\bullet}(X))=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(k[S_{1}(X)],k)
, with convolution product

(f*g)(\alpha)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in S_{2}(X)\\
d_{1}\sigma=\alpha\end{subarray}}f(d_{2}\sigma)g(d_{0}\sigma).

Here,
d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}:S_{2}(X)\rightarrow S_{1}(X)
are the face maps which pick off the faces of a
2
-simplex
\sigma
as follows:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{23.1073pt}{-6.06766pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad\qquad\begin{matrix}&d_{0}:\sigma\longmapsto\gamma\\
&d_{1}:\sigma\longmapsto\alpha\\
&d_{2}:\sigma\longmapsto\beta.\\
&\end{matrix}

As usual, write
\zeta_{X}
for the
zeta functor
and
|\zeta_{X}|
for its decategorification in
I_{\#}(X)
.

Example 3.8
.

For
X=*
, the incidence algebra
I(*)
is isomorphic to
{\tt Set}
, while the numerical incidence algebra is isomorphic to the ring of power series
k[[t]]
and the zeta function
|\zeta_{*}|\in I_{\#}(*)
corresponds to the geometric power series
(1-t)^{-1}\in k[[t]]
. Notice that in this case,

|\zeta_{*}|=(1-t)^{-\chi(*)}.

More generally, the zeta functor of a CW-complex
X
pushes forward along the terminal map
t:X\rightarrow*
to an objective linear functor
t_{*}\zeta_{X}\in I(*)
whose decategorification
|t_{*}\zeta_{X}|\in I_{\#}(*)
is the Macdonald polynomial
(1-t)^{-\chi(X)}
. For a covering map
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
, we will see that formula (
4
) is a consequence of a splitting of
\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y}
in
I(X)
.

4
Proof of the Main Theorem

For a pair of objects
X
and
Y
, let
S
and
T
denote their respective incidence algebras, as defined in Section
3
. Fix a quadratic cover
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
, interpreted appropriately in each of our four settings, and let
\pi_{*}:I(Y)\rightarrow I(X)
be the pushforward on incidence algebras induced by
\pi
.

4.1
Local Theorem

For this section, fix a “point”
x
in
X
, interpreted as:

•

a prime ideal in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
when
X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{F}
;

•

a closed point when
X
is an algebraic curve over a field;

•

a point when
X
is a finite CW complex;

•

a prime loop when
X
is a graph.

If
S=S(X)
is the simplicial set attached to
X
, let
S_{x}
be the simplicial subset “supported at
x
” , meaning:

•

when
X=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{F}
,
S
consists of simplices of powers of
x
;

•

when
X
is an algebraic curve,
S
consists of simplices of effective
0
-cycles supported at
x
;

•

when
X
is a finite CW complex,
S
consists of simplices containing
x
;

•

when
X
is a graph,
S
consists of equivalence classes of powers of
x
.

Fix a quadratic cover
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
and let
T_{x}
be the simplicial subset of
T
consisting of simplices with images in
S_{x}
. Also let
\zeta_{X,x}
and
\zeta_{Y,x}
be the zeta functions in
I(S_{x})
and
I(T_{x})
, respectively.

Theorem 4.1

(Local Version of Theorem
1.1
)
.

For each point
x
in
X
, in the incidence algebra
I(S_{x})
, there is an equivalence of linear functors

\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y,x}+\zeta_{X,x}*L_{x}(\pi)^{-}\cong\zeta_{X,x}*L_{x}(\pi)^{+}.

(15)

Proof.

The terms in formula (
15
) are defined as follows. First,
\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y,x},L_{x}(\pi)^{+}
and
L_{x}(\pi)^{-}
are represented by the spans

From here, the proof divides into cases based on the splitting type of the point
x
.

First assume
x
is a branch point of the covering
\pi
and let
y
be its (necessarily unique) preimage. In this case,
S_{x,1}
has a unique initial element
\eta
, e.g. when
X
is an algebraic curve,
\eta
is the trivial
0
-cycle
0=0x
, which we will write multiplicatively here:
\eta=x^{0}
. Take
j_{x}^{+}:S_{x,1}^{+}\hookrightarrow S_{x,1}

It is routine to check
\varphi
is a bijection (cf.
[
AK1
, Thm. 4.2]
), which proves formula (
15
) in all cases.
∎

Passing to the numerical incidence algebra
I_{\#}(S_{x})
and pushing forward to
I_{\#}(*)\cong k[[t]]
recovers a local version of formula (
7
). For example, when
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
Y=C
is a hyperelliptic curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, the objective formula pushes forward to

Z_{x}(C,t)=Z_{x}(\mathbb{P}^{1},t)(L_{x}(C,t)^{+}-L_{x}(C,t)^{-})=Z_{x}(\mathbb{P}^{1},t)L_{x}(C,t)

(16)

where
L_{x}(C,t)
is the local factor of the classical
L
-function of
C
, namely

L_{x}(C,t)=\frac{1}{1-\chi(F_{x})t^{\deg(x)}}

where
\chi
is the quadratic character associated to the extension
\mathbb{F}_{q}(C)/\mathbb{F}_{q}(t)
and
F_{x}
is the Frobenius at
x
.

Remark 4.2
.

In the proof above, we chose a labeling of the points in the fibre
\pi^{-1}(x)
. Formula (
15
) did not depend on this choice, but the bijections constructed above are nevertheless not canonical.

4.2
Global Theorem

Next, we prove the global formula of Theorem
1.1
. Keep the notation
X,Y,S,T
and
\pi
from above.

Theorem 4.3

(Theorem
1.1
)
.

In the incidence algebra
I(X)
, there is an equivalence of linear functors

\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y}+\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{-}\cong\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{+}.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

where
S_{1}^{\pm}
and
j^{\pm}
are defined below. We must then construct an isomorphism
\varphi:T_{1}\amalg B^{-}\xrightarrow{\sim}B^{+}
making the following diagram commute:
S_{1}
B^{+}
T_{1}\amalg B^{-}
*
d_{1}\circ\alpha^{+}
\pi_{1}
\varphi

.

For a
1
-simplex
\alpha\in S_{1}
, write
\alpha=\sum_{x\in X}x^{a_{x}}
. Define
S_{1}^{+}
and
S_{1}^{-}
as the following subsets of
S_{1}
:

\displaystyle S_{1}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha\in S_{1}\mid a_{x}=0\text{ for ramified }x,\sum_{x\text{ inert}}a_{x}\text{ is even}\right\}

\displaystyle S_{1}^{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{\alpha\in S_{1}\mid a_{x}=0\text{ for ramified and split }x,\sum_{x\text{ inert}}a_{x}\text{ is odd}\right\}.

Their natural inclusions
j^{+}:S_{1}^{+}\hookrightarrow S_{1}
and
j^{-}:S_{1}^{-}\hookrightarrow S_{1}
define
L(\pi)^{+}
and
L(\pi)^{-}
and the span compositions above.

We now define
\varphi:T_{1}\amalg B^{-}\xrightarrow{\sim}B^{+}
. For
\varphi=\sum_{y\in|C|}y^{b_{y}}\in T_{1}
, put
\varphi(\beta)=\tau\in S_{2}
with faces

\displaystyle d_{2}\tau

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\operatorname{supp}(\beta)\\
\text{ramified}\end{subarray}}\pi_{*}(y)^{b_{y}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\operatorname{supp}(\beta)\\
\text{split}\end{subarray}}\pi_{*}(y)^{b_{y}}

\displaystyle d_{0}\tau

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{y}\in\operatorname{supp}(\beta)\\
\text{split}\end{subarray}}\pi_{*}(\bar{y})^{b_{\bar{y}}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\operatorname{supp}(\beta)\\
\text{inert}\end{subarray}}\pi_{*}(y)^{b_{y}}

\displaystyle d_{1}\tau

\displaystyle=\pi_{*}\beta=\prod_{y\in X}\pi_{*}(y)^{b_{y}}.

Then since
d_{0}\tau
is supported away from the ramification locus of
\pi
and the exponent of
x
in
\pi_{*}(y)
is even for inert
y\mapsto x
, we see that
\tau\in B^{+}
. On the other hand, for
\sigma\in B^{-}
with
d_{2}\sigma=\alpha,d_{0}\sigma=\beta
and
d_{1}\sigma=\gamma=\alpha\beta
, define
\varphi(\sigma)=\tau
with faces

\displaystyle d_{2}\tau

\displaystyle=\prod_{x\text{ ramified}}x^{a_{x}}\prod_{x\text{ split}}x^{a_{x}}\prod_{x\text{ inert}}x^{a_{x}+1}

\displaystyle d_{0}\tau

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\operatorname{supp}(\beta)\\
\text{inert}\end{subarray}}x^{b_{x}-1}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{1}\tau=\gamma=\alpha\beta.

Then as above,
d_{0}\tau
is supported away from the ramified points in
X
and
\beta=d_{0}\sigma\in S_{1}^{-}
implies
b_{x}-1
is even, so
\tau\in B^{+}
.

To show
\varphi
is an isomorphism, we construct an inverse
\varphi^{-1}:B^{+}\rightarrow T_{1}\amalg B^{-}
as follows. If
\tau\in B^{+}
has
d_{2}\tau=\alpha,d_{0}=\beta
and
d_{1}\tau=\gamma=\alpha\beta
, with
\alpha
supported away from all inert points in
X
, send it to

\varphi^{-1}(\tau)=\sum_{x\text{ ramified}}y^{c_{x}}\prod_{x\text{ split}}y^{a_{x}}\bar{y}^{b_{x}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\text{ inert}\\
b_{x}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}y^{b_{x}/2}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\text{ inert}\\
b_{x}\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}y^{b_{x}}\;\in T_{1}.

If
\alpha
is supported on any inert points, send
\tau
to
\beta^{-1}(\tau)=\sigma\in B^{-}
with faces

\displaystyle d_{2}\sigma

\displaystyle=\prod_{x\text{ ramified}}x^{a_{x}}\prod_{x\text{ split}}x^{a_{x}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\operatorname{supp}(\alpha)\\
\text{ inert}\end{subarray}}x^{a_{x}-1}

\displaystyle d_{0}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\text{ ramified}}x^{b_{x}}\prod_{x\text{ split}}x^{b_{x}}\prod_{x\text{ inert}}x^{b_{x}+1}

\displaystyle d_{1}\sigma

\displaystyle=d_{1}\tau=\gamma.

By construction,
\varphi
is a bijection and it satisfies
d_{1}\circ\varphi=\pi\amalg d_{1}
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Recall that for each split point
x\in X
, we are choosing a labeling
\pi^{-1}(x)=\{y,\bar{y}\}
in order to write down the formulas in the above proof. This implies that the isomorphism
\varphi
is not canonical: it depends on a choice of section of
\pi
over the split locus.

Remark 4.5
.

Taking the cardinality of the formula in Theorem
4.3
recovers formulas (
2
), (
3
), (
4
) and (
6
), so Theorem
4.3
can be considered a categorification of those zeta function formulas. Explicitly, cardinality is a map from the objective incidence algebra
I(X)
to the reduced numerical incidence algebra
I_{\#}(X)
:

\displaystyle|\cdot|:I(X)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow I_{\#}(X)

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\biggl(|f|:\alpha\mapsto|v^{-1}(\alpha)|\biggr).

Applying
|\cdot|
to the formula
\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y}+\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{-}\cong\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{+}
produces a formula which, upon further applying one of the “generating function maps” from Sections
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.8
and
3.4
, recovers formulas (
2
), (
3
), (
4
) and (
6
), respectively.

Remark 4.6
.

When
X
is the ring of integers in a number field, an algebraic curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
or a finite graph, it is possible to formulate a version of Theorem
4.3
in the “full” incidence algebra of the poset
S=Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)
, rather than treating it as a monoid. This corresponds to first taking the decalage of the monoid structure, then applying the same techniques as above. We have elected to omit further discussion of these formulas since they don’t appear to have practical applications. Nevertheless, they provide new relations not just among effective
0
-cycles in a cover (and therefore points), but also intervals of effective
0
-cycles (and therefore families of points).

Remark 4.7
.

The
X=C/\mathbb{F}_{q}
case of Theorem
4.3
generalizes easily to arbitrary double covers of algebraic curves
C\rightarrow D
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, giving interesting formulas for their zeta functions in
k[[t]]
:

Z(C,t)=Z(D,t)L(C/D,t)

(17)

for an appropriate “relative
L
-function”
L(C/D,t)
. The novelty of Theorem
4.3
is that it expresses this relation directly in the incidence algebra of
D
(objectively or numerically), which is stronger than (
17
) even in the case when
D=\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

4.3
General Topological Covers

Beyond topological double covers, which are already handled by Theorem
4.3
, we can use objective linear algebra to categorify formula (
4
) for ramified covers of any degree.

First, recall formula (
10
), which says that for an unbranched cover
Y\rightarrow X
of degree
n
,
\chi(Y)=n\chi(X)
. Let’s lift this formula to an objective formula in an incidence algebra.

As in Section
3.8
, let
S_{\bullet}(X)
(resp.
S_{\bullet}(Y)
) be the simplicial complex associated to
X
(resp.
Y
) and let
S(\pi):S_{\bullet}(Y)\rightarrow S_{\bullet}(X)
be the simplicial map induced by
\pi
. This induces a map of incidence algebras
\pi_{*}:I(Y)\rightarrow I(X)
.

In dimension
0
, a degree
n
cover
Y\rightarrow*
is just a set of
n
points
Y=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}
, so formula (
10
) in this case is the decategorification of

\zeta_{Y}\cong\underbrace{\zeta_{*}*\cdots*\zeta_{*}}_{n}.

This works because points have no nondegenerate simplices of higher dimension.

Unfortunately, this only applies to
0
-simplices. The Möbius function is better suited to computing Euler characteristic objectively. For any
X
, the zeta functor in
I(S_{\bullet}(X))
is

[

\zeta_{X}^{(0)}=\delta_{X}
, the unit of convolution in

, and define

\Phi_{even}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\zeta_{X}^{(2k)}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{odd}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\zeta_{X}^{(2k+1)}.

These functors satisfy an objective form of Möbius inversion
[
GKT2
, Thm. 3.8]
:

\zeta_{X}*\Phi_{even}\cong\delta+\zeta_{X}*\Phi_{odd}.

In the numerical incidence algebra
I_{\#}(S_{\bullet}(X))
, this formula descends to

\delta=\zeta_{X}*(\Phi_{even}-\Phi_{odd})=\zeta_{X}*\mu_{X}

where
\mu_{X}
is the numerical Möbius function for
S_{\bullet}(X)
.

Define spaces

Proposition 4.8
.

If
X
is a finite CW complex, then
\chi(X)=|E_{even}(X)|-|E_{odd}(X)|
.

{

{{{{{}}{

{
{}{}{}}{}{

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.8 27.56 L 30.81 8.56}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.70717}{-0.70705}{0.70705}{0.70717}{22.26898pt}{6.18463pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.66 2.21 C -1.52 1.38 0 0.14 0.42 0 C 0 -0.14 -1.52 -1.38 -1.66 -2.21}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right).

In this way, the construction can be interpreted as an “objective Macdonald polynomial”: pushing forward
\Phi_{even}(X)
and
\Phi_{odd}(X)
along the terminal map
t:X\rightarrow*
produces linear functors
t_{*}\Phi_{even}(X),t_{*}\Phi_{odd}(X)\in I(S_{\bullet}(*))
and, as discussed above, their decategorifications in
I_{\#}(S_{\bullet}(*))\cong k[[t]]
are

|t_{*}\Phi_{even}(X)|=(1-t)^{-|E_{even}(X)|}\quad\text{and}\quad|t_{*}\Phi_{odd}(X)|=(1-t)^{-|E_{odd}(X)|}

so that

\frac{|t_{*}\Phi_{even}(X)|}{|t_{*}\Phi_{odd}(X)|}=(1-t)^{-|E_{even}(X)|+|E_{odd}(X)|}=(1-t)^{-\chi(X)}.

Theorem 4.9
.

For a degree
n
cover
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
of finite CW complexes, there is an equivalence of linear functors

\pi_{*}\Phi_{even}(Y)*\Phi_{odd}(X)^{n}\cong\pi_{*}\Phi_{odd}(Y)*\Phi_{even}(X)^{n}

in the objective incidence algebra
I(S_{\bullet}(X))
. Here,
(-)^{n}
denotes the
n
-fold convolution.

Proof.

Each nondegenerate
k
-cell in
X
lifts to exactly
n
nondegenerate
k
-cells in
Y
. This gives us bijections
E_{even}(Y)\xrightarrow{\sim}\coprod_{i=1}^{n}E_{even}(X)
and
E_{odd}(Y)\xrightarrow{\sim}\coprod_{i=1}^{n}E_{odd}(X)
which assemble into the stated formula.
∎

Pushing forward to the incidence algebra of a point and taking cardinalities recovers formula (
10
) via generating functions:

(1-t)^{-\chi(Y)}=\frac{|t_{*}\Phi_{even}(Y)|}{|t_{*}\Phi_{odd}(Y)|}=\left(\frac{|t_{*}\Phi_{even}(X)|}{|t_{*}\Phi_{odd}(X)|}\right)^{n}=(1-t)^{-n\chi(X)}.

Theorem 4.10
.

For a connected, degree
n
branched cover
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
, there is a linear functor
R\in I(S_{\bullet}(Y))
and an equivalence

\pi_{*}\Phi_{even}(Y)*\Phi_{odd}(X)^{n}*\pi_{*}R\cong\pi_{*}\Phi_{odd}(Y)*\Phi_{even}(X)^{n}

in
I(S_{\bullet}(X))
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we give the proof in the case when
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
is a branched cover of surfaces. Let
X_{br}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
be the branch locus of
\pi
and for each
x_{i}
, write
\pi^{-1}(x_{i})=\{y_{i1},\ldots,y_{ik_{i}}\}
for each
1\leq i\leq r
. Each nondegenerate
k
-cell
\sigma\in S_{k}(X)
lifts to
n
nondegenerate
k
-cells in
S_{k}(Y)

except
when when
k=0
and
\sigma=x_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq r
. In this case, there are
n-k_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}(e(y_{ij})-1)
points “missing” from the count. Said another way, there is a bijection

\coprod_{i=1}^{n}E_{even}(X)\xrightarrow{\sim}E_{even}(Y)\amalg\{z_{i1},\ldots,z_{i,n-k_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{r}

where
z_{i1},\ldots,z_{i,n-k_{i}}
are these “missing points” in
\pi^{-1}(x_{i})
. Setting
R=\{z_{i1},\ldots,z_{i,n-k_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{r}
, we obtain the stated formula.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

Let
G
be the group of deck transformations of
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
. Then it should be possible to lift formula (
11
) to an equivalence of linear functors of
G
-representations, eliminating the need to separate
\Phi_{even}
and
\Phi_{odd}
in the above formulation. See Appendix
A.3
.

Question 4.12
.

Is it also possible to prove
\chi(X\times Y)=\chi(X)\chi(Y)
this way? And more generally
\chi(E)=\chi(B)\chi(F)
for a fibration
F\rightarrow E\rightarrow B
?

4.4
A Motivic Formula

In the case of a cover of curves
Y\rightarrow X
over a field
k
, Theorem
4.3
can be generalized to a motivic formula by replacing the decomposition sets
Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)
and
Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(Y)
with the
decomposition schemes

S^{\bullet}X
and
S^{\bullet}Y
, where

S^{n}X=\coprod_{(j_{0},\ldots,j_{n-1})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Sym}^{j_{i}}X

with the simplicial scheme structure induced by the simplicial structure on the indices (i.e. the additive monoid
\mathbb{N}_{0}
). That is,
S^{\bullet}X
is the decomposition scheme attached to the monoid scheme
\coprod_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Sym}^{n}X
. A variant of the proof of Theorem
4.3
gives the following formula.

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
\pi:Y\rightarrow X
be a double cover of varieties over a field
k
and let
I_{mot}(X)
be the incidence algebra of the decomposition scheme
S^{\bullet}X
. Then in
I_{mot}(X)
, there is an equivalence of linear functors

\pi_{*}\zeta_{Y}+\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{-}\cong\zeta_{X}*L(\pi)^{+}.

Proof.

Viewing the scheme

S^{1}X=\coprod_{n=0}^{\infty}\operatorname{Sym}^{n}X

via its functor of points, we can extend the definitions of
S_{1}^{+}
and
S_{1}^{-}
from Theorem
4.3
functorially to subschemes
(S^{1}X)^{+},(S^{1}X)^{-}\subseteq S^{1}X
which determine spans
L(\pi)^{+}
and
L(\pi)^{-}
representing linear functors in the incidence algebra
I(X)
. The rest of the proof goes through as before, by the Yoneda lemma.
∎

This in turn determines an analogous formula in
R
for each motivic measure
\mu:K_{0}({\tt Var}_{k})\rightarrow R
, where
K_{0}({\tt Var}_{k})
is the Grothendieck ring of
k
-varieties. Several examples include:

•

k=\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
R=\mathbb{Z}
,
\mu=\#(-)(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, the point-counting measure over the finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Since
\operatorname{Sym}^{n}X(\mathbb{F}_{q})=Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(X)_{n}
, the set of effective
0
-cycles of degree
n
, this decategorification recovers Theorem
4.3
.

•

k=\mathbb{C}
,
R=\mathbb{Z}
,
\mu=
Euler characteristic, which recovers formula (
4
) for Riemann surfaces.

•

R=\mathbb{Z}[SB]
, the ring of stable birational equivalence classes, and
\mu=
the Larsen–Lunts measure
[
LL
]
.

•

R=K_{0}({\tt Var}_{k})
,
\mu=\operatorname{id}
, giving a universal version of (
7
) in
K_{0}({\tt Var}_{k})
. Also compare this to the approach in
[
DH
]
.

5
Applications

5.1
Point Counts on Elliptic Curves

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
L
-polynomial
L(E,t)=1-a_{q}(E)t+qt^{2}
where
a_{q}(E)=q+1-\#E(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. On one hand, formula (
3
) says that

Z(E,t)=Z(\mathbb{P}^{1},t)L(E,t).

On the other hand, Theorems
4.1
and
4.3
, through Remark
4.5
, show that
L(E,t)=L(E,t)^{+}-L(E,t)^{-}
can be written as

L(E,t)=\prod_{x\in|\mathbb{P}^{1}|}(1-\chi(x)t^{\deg(x)})^{-1}

where
\chi
is the following quadratic character:

\chi(x)=\begin{cases}0,&x\text{ is ramified}\\
1,&x\text{ is split}\\
-1,&x\text{ is inert}\end{cases}

which can be extended multiplicatively to all effective
0
-cycles.

Let’s spell this out explicitly. The
t^{n}
coefficient of
L(E,t)^{+}
is the cardinality of the fibre of
j^{+}:S_{1}^{+}\rightarrow S_{1}
over the set
S_{1}(n)
of
0
-cycles of degree
n
in
S_{1}
:
S_{1}
S_{1}(n)
S_{1}^{+}
S_{1}(n)^{+}
j^{+}

.

Then
\#S_{1}(n)^{+}
is equal to the number of effective
0
-cycles
\alpha=\sum_{x}a_{x}x
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
n
with
a_{x}=0
for ramified
x
and the sum of the
a_{x}
even for inert
x
;
\#S_{1}(n)^{-}
is defined similarly. Putting these together,

\#S_{1}(n)^{+}-\#S_{1}(n)^{-}=\sum_{\deg(\alpha)=n}\chi(\alpha).

This allows us to reinterpret the
L
-polynomial in terms of the character
\chi
, as claimed. Write

\prod_{x\in|\mathbb{P}^{1}|}(1-\chi(x)t^{\deg(x)})^{-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f(n)t^{n}\quad\text{where}\quad f(n)=\sum_{\deg(\alpha)=n}\chi(\alpha).

As an element of the numerical incidence algebra
I_{\#}(Z_{0}^{\operatorname{eff}}(\mathbb{P}^{1}))
,
f
is the sequence

f(0)=1,\quad f(1)=-a_{q}(E),\quad f(2)=q\quad\text{and}\quad f(n)=0\text{ for }n>2.

For
n=1
, this implies the first part of formula (
8
):

a_{q}(E)=-\sum_{x\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\chi(x)=\#\{\text{inert }x\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\}-\#\{\text{split }x\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\}=:i_{q}(E)-s_{q}(E)

(18)

while for
n=2
, we obtain

q=\sum_{\deg(\alpha)=2}\chi(\alpha).

Formula (
3
) then implies

\#\operatorname{Sym}^{2}E(\mathbb{F}_{q})=(q^{2}+q+1)-(q+1)a_{q}+\sum_{\deg(\alpha)=2}\chi(\alpha).

(19)

A similar proof shows the rest of formula (
8
):

\#\operatorname{Sym}^{n}E(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\sum_{i+j=n}(q^{i}+\ldots+q+1)\sum_{\deg(\alpha)=j}\chi(\alpha).

(20)

These are the recursions which generate all point counts
\#E(\mathbb{F}_{q^{r}})
from knowledge of just
\#E(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. (To go from the sequence
\#\operatorname{Sym}^{n}E(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to the sequence
\#E(\mathbb{F}_{q^{n}})
, take ghost components, or equivalently, apply the logarithmic derivative to
Z(E,t)
.) Our proof demonstrates that they come directly from an objective
L
-function and, more importantly, that this
L
-function enumerates points (weighted by
\chi
)
on the projective line
.

Example 5.1
.

Consider the elliptic curve
E/\mathbb{F}_{5}

[
LMFDB
,
Abelian Variety 1.5.d
]
with Weierstrass equation

E:y^{2}=x^{3}+x+1.

It is known that
\#E(\mathbb{F}_{5})=9
(see below), giving
L(E,t)=1+3t+5t^{2}
. Projecting to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, there are
3
more split points than inert points. Since
x^{3}+x+1
is irreducible over
\mathbb{F}_{5}
,
\infty
is the only ramified point on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Split points contribute
2
rational points on
E
, so there must be exactly
4
split points and
1
inert point downstairs. Here’s a table of the closed points of
E
with images in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{5})
, organized by splitting type of their images.

point

ramified image

point

split image

point

inert image

O

\infty

(0,1)

0

\left(1,\sqrt{3}\right)

1

(0,4)

\left(1,-\sqrt{3}\right)

(2,1)

2

(2,4)

(3,1)

3

(3,4)

(4,2)

4

(4,3)

The
8
split points cover the
4
split points on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
identified above. The inert points
\left(1,\pm\sqrt{3}\right)
cover the inert point
1\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{5})
, with
\sqrt{3}
a fixed square root of
3
in
\mathbb{F}_{25}
. Moreover, formula (
19
) quickly allows us to compute that
\#E(\mathbb{F}_{25})=27
. We can further deduce then that in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{25})
, there are
12
split points, lifting to
24
of the
27
points in
E(\mathbb{F}_{25})
, and
13
inert points. Similarly,
\#E(\mathbb{F}_{125})=108
and the
126
points in
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{F}_{125})
consist of
4
ramified points,
31
split points and
91
inert points.

Example 5.2
.

Up to isogeny, the elliptic curve
E
in Example
5.1
has a unique quadratic twist over
\mathbb{F}_{5}

[
LMFDB
,
Abelian Variety 1.5.ad
]
,

E^{\prime}:y^{2}=x^{3}+4x+2

with
\#E^{\prime}(\mathbb{F}_{5})=3
and
L(E^{\prime},t)=1-3t+5t^{2}
. This time, there are
3
more inert points than split points, from which we can deduce that on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, there is a single ramification point at
\infty
,
4
inert points and
1
split point. Here’s the corresponding table of points for
E^{\prime}
:

\left(2,\sqrt{3}\right)
